cross Amplifying Latency and Cost of LLM Inference via Rowhammer Attack

Authors: Mrityunjay Shukla, Shubham Roy, Biswabandan Panda

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) achieve high inference efficiency using optimized GPU kernels generated by just-in-time (JIT) compilation frameworks such as Triton. Triton compiles GPU kernels through an MLIR/LLVM-based pipeline. We investigate whether a Rowhammer-induced bit-flip in the CPU's DRAM can degrade GPU-based LLM inference performance while preserving output correctness. We present LLVMhammer, the first Rowhammer attack that causes LLM inference slowdown by inducing a bit-flip in Triton's compilation pipeline. A bit-flip in a performance-critical optimization pass causes Triton to generate a functionally identical but suboptimal GPU kernel. Since Triton caches the generated kernel on disk, a single successful bit-flip causes persistent slowdown across subsequent inference executions. Triton's target-independent stages are shared across GPU vendors, which makes our attack GPU vendor-agnostic. To identify exploitable bit-flip locations without manual inspection, we build Karnage, a tool that isolates useful bit-flip locations in Triton's target-independent compilation pipeline. We evaluate LLVMHammer across a diverse set of LLMs, including Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3, and Deepseek-llm-7B-chat, across NVIDIA RTX 4090 and AMD RX 7900 XTX GPUs. LLVMhammer increases inference latency of an LLM inference server running on a single GPU by 4-16\%, which, in turn, can translate into additional annual operating costs at the scale of multi-GPU deployments. We further show that LLVMhammer can also corrupt LLM outputs across different GPU vendors, reducing BERTScore to as low as 25\%, illustrating the broader security implications of bit-flips in Triton's compilation pipeline.

cross What Lies Beyond Perception: A Partition Theory of the Limits of AI Evaluation and Oversight

Authors: Ahmed M. Mahran

Abstract: Replacing the judge can move a benchmark score, and models can be trained to hide capabilities. Both put the evaluator in question: what can it certify? We develop a partition theory of evaluation from one principle: judgement is bounded by perception. An agent's discrimination is the partition of its inputs into classes it cannot tell apart. Discrimination is necessary for capability but not sufficient, so bounds on it bear directly on whether evaluation can show that a model lacks an ability. We prove a one-sided bound. An examinee whose partition refines its evaluator's has the discrimination to pass every test the evaluator can grade, provided each input has an acceptable reply. We define the echo test, which accepts any reply the evaluator perceives as identical to the input, and show that it is complete for the tests the evaluator can grade. Passing therefore certifies at most a floor on discrimination, never a ceiling, and only a sharper evaluator can raise it. Used to detect concealment, echo-test failure points the wrong way: under exhaustive probing it flags every honestly weaker examinee and no concealer that presents as the evaluator's peer. Exact imitation of a weaker model is undetectable by any behavioural evaluator. We extend the bound to ensembles of monitors and to channels a model controls, explain published findings on judge disagreement and sandbagging, and derive testable predictions. A safety case that argues from behavioural evaluation alone that a model lacks an ability must assume the model's cooperation.

cross xVGAE: A Hierarchical Variational Graph Autoencoder for Exchangeable Graphs

Authors: Daniele Micheletti, Federica Zoe Ricci, Erik B. Sudderth

Abstract: Graphs play a crucial role in many applications, ranging from drug discovery to social network modeling and astronomy. Generative models for graphs have substantially advanced in recent years, but we identify relatively simple scenarios where state-of-the-art models struggle to converge within a standard compute budget, or exhibit prohibitively slow generation times. We propose a generative architecture that achieves quality competitive with state-of-the-art models and is orders of magnitude faster at generation. Our approach is inspired by the Aldous-Hoover theorem, a classic representation theorem that characterizes any probability distribution of graphs that is invariant to permutations of node indices or exchangeable. This theorem establishes that three ingredients suffice: a graph-level latent variable, a set of exchangeable node-specific latent variables, and a so-called graphon function that maps graph- and node-level variables to the probability of an edge between any node pair. Given a training set of graphs, our exchangeable Variational Graph AutoEncoder (xVGAE) learns an approximation to their underlying graphons, as well as graph- and node-level latent variables. We show that our xVGAE encodes latent representations that correlate with graph- and node-level properties, and can (quickly, in one shot) generate new graphs that closely match key training set statistics.

cross Semi-Linear Gaussian Bayesian Networks: Enhancing Flexibility in Node Dependencies via penalized B-Splines

Authors: Aitor Oviedo-Madrid, Mabel Morales-Otero, Jose Gonzalez-Gomariz, Ruben Arma\~nanzas

Abstract: Bayesian networks for continuous variables often rely on restrictive assumptions: linear Gaussian models lack flexibility to capture nonlinear dependencies, while non-parametric approaches provide greater flexibility at a high computational cost. This creates a gap for modeling scenarios where variables are approximately Gaussian but exhibit nonlinear relationships. We introduce Semi-Linear Gaussian Bayesian Networks (SLGBN), a novel class of continuous Bayesian networks designed to bridge this gap, preserving Gaussian conditional distributions while introducing nonlinear conditional mean functions. The proposed framework provides a unified probabilistic framework combining linear Gaussian and nonlinear Gaussian conditional models within the same Bayesian network Nonlinear conditional mean functions are estimated using generalized additive models (GAMs) with penalized B-splines. We present parameter estimation procedures for both types of nodes, as well as a greedy hill-climbing algorithm for learning the network structure under the SLGBN framework. The results show that, in the presence of nonlinear Gaussian relationships, SLGBN achieves better fit and improved structure recovery compared to alternative models, particularly when combined with the Extended Bayesian Information Criterion (EBIC*) score. Furthermore, by retaining Gaussian conditional distributions, SLGBN offers computational advantages over fully semiparametric approaches.

cross Continuous-depth Deep Gaussian Processes

Authors: Ying Li, Sanyou Wu, Zi Yang, Qiaochu Xu, Yuhao Liu, Zhidi Lin, Michael Minyi Zhang, Petar Djuric, Alexandre H. Thiery

Abstract: Deep Gaussian processes (DGPs) are appealing Bayesian regression models, but their standard discrete-depth parameterization often becomes harder to optimize as depth grows. We argue that depth is better treated as a latent continuous evolution than as a long discrete stack. We therefore propose a continuous-depth formulation in which the latent representation follows a stochastic differential equation with a depth-indexed drift family equipped with a GP-based prior. This reframes the core design choice as the prior placed on that family, rather than adopting a purely neural continuous-depth parameterization as in neural ODE or neural SDE models. We study two instantiations of this idea: CDGP, a direct GP prior over the state--depth drift, and FlowDGP, a flow-evolved prior that captures richer depth dependence at lower training cost. To motivate the redesign, we analyze how the single-sample DGP objective can become increasingly sensitive under repeated layer composition, while residual DGP is a useful first repair that still remains a finite discrete construction. We also give pathwise sensitivity analysis for the continuous-depth formulation. Experiments on synthetic, benchmark, and large-scale regression tasks show that residual DGP improves over plain DGP, while CDGP and FlowDGP provide a stronger overall story across the regimes we study.

cross Equilibria and Learning in Competitive Market Making

Authors: Matteo Onger, Luigi Foscari

Abstract: We propose a market-making game played among $N \ge 2$ strategic market makers and a single non-strategic representative trader. cross Parameter Learning in Active Inference via Variational Message Passing

Authors: Benedikt Waldeck

Abstract: Active inference casts perception, learning and planning as variational inference, where the Expected Free Energy (EFE) defines the planning objective. Recent work showed that EFE minimization can be written as Variational Free Energy (VFE) minimization on a generative model augmented with epistemic priors, and that the resulting entropy-corrected objective is minimized by sum-product message passing (SP-MP) on modified factor functions. These formulations treat model uncertainty as inference over a finite set of known models. We extend them to planning during learning, where beliefs over the observation and transition parameters $\theta_A$, $\theta_B$ are Dirichlet priors. Closed-form Dirichlet updates are usually obtained by a structured mean-field factorization between parameters and states, but under exactly that factorization the information-gain corrections vanish. We resolve this by giving every factor its own parameter prior, frozen during a rollout. Conjugacy then makes each factor belief a product of a marginal over the categorical variables and a one-step Bayes posterior of the parameters. This reintroduces the datum-dependent parameter posteriors that mean-field factorization destroys. We show that evaluating the epistemic priors under the resulting Bethe joint recovers the information gains. Stationarity of this single closed-form objective yields SP-MP whose factor functions are augmented by the information-gain weight. Experiments on two grid POMDPs, in which only a planner that learns the model finds the safe route, show that the method plans to efficiently learn $\theta_A$ and $\theta_B$. It reaches the goal on every episode, whereas SP-MP planners with the same inference update plateau or take the risky route.

Authors: Caixing Wang, Yue Wang

Abstract: Random projections (RP) offer an effective method for reducing computational and storage overhead while preserving the geometric structure of data. However, existing studies have primarily focused on the optimal generalization performance of specific kernel-regularized algorithms with RP in the well-specified setting and under restrictive conditions. In this paper, we provide a comprehensive and improved analysis of the generalization performance of RP-based spectral algorithms under general conditions \change{without sacrificing statistical efficiency}. Authors: Tardelli Ronan Coelho Stekel

Abstract: Many language-model pipelines end in a closed-set decision, such as selecting a tool. Prompting a large language model for it is slow and order-sensitive. We ask how small an encoder for such decisions can be and whether it should read the options jointly or separately. In a three-seed study at 32 million parameters, per-option scoring beats joint scoring on a held-out sample of a 37-benchmark decision suite by 5.8 points of chance-corrected skill at matched optimizer steps and by 2.8 at approximately matched training compute; two external benchmarks agree, though not significantly on their unseen tasks. Crossing reader with initialisation shows that the advantage requires a reranker initialisation, whose query-candidate pair input matches per-option reading; from the raw encoder the two readers are indistinguishable. Per-option scoring needs 5 to 13 times more encoder passes: at most twice the median latency, but more than four times the mean. Of six released joint encoders (17 million to 1 billion parameters), the 68- and 150-million-parameter models exceed a 435-million-parameter DeBERTa-v3 zero-shot classifier by 4.9 and 5.9 accuracy points on 209 overlap-audited System1-Arena tasks and are at least on par with it on the 145 tasks unseen in training. The smallest answers a median request in 14 milliseconds on 16 processor threads, against 728 for the classifier. Reordering options changes 12 to 23% of the joint models' answers (per-option scoring: none). Up to 68 million parameters, they lead their size range on a public leaderboard's private, mainly in-distribution tests.

cross Diagnostic Loss: Asymmetric Supervision for Radiology Report Generation

Authors: Ignacy Kolton, Wenxuan Li, Zongwei Zhou, Jaros{\l}aw B. \'Cwik{\l}a, Arkadiusz Sitek, Pedro R. A. S. Bassi, Szymon P{\l}otka

Abstract: Automated radiology report generation requires models to do more than produce fluent clinical narratives: they must explicitly state supported diagnoses while avoiding unsupported ones. However, existing medical vision-language models (MedVLMs) are typically trained with token-level maximum likelihood objectives, which prioritize text agreement over diagnostically critical terms. As a result, models can miss key diagnoses and introduce wrong ones. We identify this as an objective misalignment between conventional language modeling and the asymmetric diagnostic requirements of radiology reporting. To encode these requirements, we introduce Diagnostic Loss (DLoss), a differentiable, architecture-agnostic objective that operates directly on decoder token probabilities. DLoss separately aggregates probability mass over vocabularies associated with diagnoses supported by the reference report and with unsupported alternatives. Using asymmetric pooling across token positions in the report, it encourages supported diagnoses to receive sufficient probability in at least one position while suppressing spurious probability spikes for unsupported diagnoses across all positions during training. The loss can be applied to existing 3D MedVLMs without modifying their vision backbones, language decoders, or inference procedures, and introduces no additional inference-time computation. We train and evaluate DLoss on 50,000 abdominal CT scans across several 3D MedVLM architectures on both in-distribution and out-of-distribution report-generation benchmarks. Across these settings, DLoss improves diagnostic faithfulness while maintaining competitive performance on conventional language-generation metrics, showing the value of asymmetric sequence-level supervision for radiology report generation. The code for DLoss is available at https://github.com/gmum/DLoss.

URLs: https://github.com/gmum/DLoss.

cross Mostly Reading: What Open-Book Scores Measure in Document-Level LoRA Adapters

Authors: Martino M. L. Pulici, Cuong Xuan Chu, Evgeny Kharlamov, Volker Tresp

Abstract: Single-document LoRA adapters have been proposed as units of knowledge to be merged and retrieved; yet open-book scoring, with the document in the prompt, can credit a document's own adapter (its specialist) for reading that document as readily as for holding it. Training one adapter for each of 150 Wikipedia articles on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, we find that open-book scores mostly measure a reading behaviour every adapter shares: a random adapter trained on another document recovers 82 % of the specialist's open-book advantage over the base model but only 2 % of its advantage on a cloze probe that asks for the same document's facts without providing the source text, a share we cannot distinguish from zero. Content does reach the weights, since the specialists beat the base model in cloze recall by 0.094, and the geometry of their updates follows the documents they read; yet, in a test of 30 queries, adapters chosen by weight-space proximity or text embedding are not distinguishable from random ones on either probe, although that test cannot exclude an advantage of about four fifths of what a query's own adapter gains over a random one. At 16 documents or more, correctly scaled merges recover 87 % to 89 % of the specialists' open-book advantage but only 12 % to 16 % of their cloze advantage, with intervals that run from below zero to at most 32 %, against 94 % for joint training.We also report four silent errors, each of which produced a clean, wrong result.

cross NN-Based Transistor Modeling for Ultra-Fast SPICE Simulation and Standard Cell Library Characterization

Authors: Rodion Novkin, Hussam Amrouch

Abstract: Neural network (NN)-based transistor models have proven to be a promising solution to accelerate device modeling. Although these models demonstrated remarkable speedup in circuit simulations, they were not rigorously tested in complex tasks within standard EDA tool flows for chip design, which require very high transistor model precision to provide accurate results and converge properly. To investigate their full potential and promise, we develop NN-based transistor models that accurately predict the drain current and various charges for a 3nm nanosheet, and employ the models for a standard cell library characterization. This enables us to evaluate the accuracy and speedup under a very wide range of SPICE simulations, benchmarking our models against an industry-standard implementation. Since standard cell library characterization involves thousands of SPICE simulations, it serves as a great benchmark for transistor model quality and consistency. To further evaluate model accuracy, we also perform analog- and digital-level simulations for various complex circuits. Our experiments reveal that NN-based models are a valid alternative to existing transistor compact models, capable of producing accurate delay and power estimates with a much higher speed and convergence time compared to the conventional Verilog-A-based industry-standard (BSIM-CMG) transistor compact model. Our NNs achieve sub-0.11% and 0.76% errors for delay and total power evaluation on a wide range of circuits, while taking up to 8 times less time during the standard cell library characterization. Additionally, we provide comprehensive information and guidance on NN accuracy, unveiling the relationship between NN size and its effect on circuit simulation precision.

cross DRAFT: Decoding and Reconstructing 3D Room Layouts from fMRI and Camera Trajectories

Authors: Chi-Wei Lee, An-Kai Chen, Cheng-Han Chiang, Yi-Chun Chang, Po-Chih Kuo

Abstract: While functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) decoders reconstruct images and 3D objects, recovering the metric geometry of rooms from a moving viewpoint remains an open challenge. We introduce, to our knowledge for the first time, the task of reconstructing 3D layouts of held-out scenes using only the known camera trajectories and the fMRI signals of participants watching indoor walkthroughs, without any visual frames. The task is hard because of the temporal mismatch between rapid visual changes and blurred fMRI signals. Our method, DRAFT (Decoding and Reconstructing 3D room lAyouts from fMRI and Trajectories), extracts a compact layout representation from the early visual cortex and fuses this neural code with the geometric free space derived from camera motion. To evaluate it, we propose layout r, the Pearson correlation between predicted and ray-cast log depths. On ten held-out scenes (20 participants), DRAFT reaches a layout r of 0.480, outperforming the camera path alone (0.446) and seven fMRI decoders (at most 0.429). DRAFT also works as a plug-in that improves all seven decoders without retraining, raising their layout r from 0.171-0.429 to 0.487-0.493. Brain activity and camera motion can thus recover the metric layout of unseen rooms, a first step toward reconstructing the 3D spaces people see.

cross Delta N-gram: Forward-Only Continual Writing into a Language Model's Native N-gram Memory

Authors: Hao Wu

Abstract: Our previous analysis found that a model's native hashed N-gram table is where continual updates disturb the base least, but its writes needed backpropagation and changed the pretrained table. We present Delta N-gram, a forward-only write path into that table. The pretrained table T^0 is never modified. Evidence is written into a sparse patch \Delta on the rows it addresses: a 4.1M-parameter writer emits a target and a gate per (position, hash head) event, and a block-averaged delta rule keeps each row a convex combination of its old value and the gated targets. The writer is trained offline on raw text with the pretraining objective; at use time writing takes forward passes only, and clearing \Delta restores the original model. A 0.6B prototype trained to answer questions keeps 16/16 facts after a second write; online gradient writes keep 2--4/16. We study two writers: a plain writer that encodes evidence with the T^0-only model, and a delta writer that reads its current memory while encoding. On Qwen3.8-Flash-Next, the plain writer's memory is document-specific: on 64 held-out news events it lowers continuation NLL by 0.068 nats/token against empty memory and by 0.024 against another document's memory, and 0.063 and 0.013 after 16 intervening articles. The delta writer's memory is more document-specific and keeps more of it under distraction (70% vs 54%). Over 35,000 writes on our previous seven-task chain both writers stay stable and the delta writer drifts less (layer-2 math drift 176% vs 188%); later accuracy changes by -0.26/-0.52pp (-7.29pp for gradient updates) and small-k_i drift is 17.9/17.5% (39.2%). Acquisition is negative, and general-input drift reaches 32.8% (20.75%): format rows read by every example grow to 4--12 times their pretrained norm. Zeroing eight such rows removes over 98% of layer-2 drift on math inputs, together with their NLL gain.

cross Post-Audit Mirage: Identifiability Limits of Offline Agent Verification

Authors: Aman Behera, Payal Payal

Abstract: Offline tests can fail to reveal whether an agent update will remain safe after deployment. We operationalize this post-audit mirage using matched safe and harmful lifecycles that expose identical verifier-visible evidence while an isolated scorer records opposite deployment truth. Our theorem applies standard observational-equivalence and indistinguishability ideas to this executable audit boundary and states the resulting error-abstention tradeoff. The Post-Audit Mirage benchmark makes that boundary executable across authorization, scheduling, and shared-capacity environments. IdentifiedRangeMonitor uses fresh group-labelled harm outcomes to return deploy, hold, cannot determine, or unsupported. Experiments show that monitoring can recover the missing distinction in favorable settings but may remain undecided for rare groups, small effects, or missing observations. Verifier-agent evaluation must therefore test both calculation validity and whether the available evidence supports the deployment decision.

cross Mixing Matters: Evidence Position Bias Across Sequence Mixers in Long-Context Question Answering

Authors: Aman Behera, Namit Solanki, Mehul Anand

Abstract: Long-context models often use answer-bearing evidence less effectively when it appears away from a prompt boundary. We compare this position bias across Transformer, state-space, and hybrid Mamba architectures by moving one gold document through a fixed set of nine distractors. The evaluated Transformers show a beginning advantage that the evaluated state-space models lack, while both families favor evidence near the end. A matched pure-versus-hybrid Mamba comparison points in the same direction but remains statistically uncertain. The family difference appears among models that can answer the task. Transformer primacy reappears on synthetic retrieval, where state-space ceiling prevents a family comparison. A corpus swap within one state-space architecture changes overall accuracy without a detectable change in curve shape. Prompt changes can create or nearly remove a beginning advantage. Position remains decodable across architectures even when response curves differ, and attention concentration near initial tokens covaries with beginning sensitivity in the tested Transformer family. These exploratory results show that effective context use depends on the evaluated architecture family, capability, task, and prompt, without isolating the sequence mixer as the sole cause.

cross GM-GReFEL: Geometry-Guided Spatiotemporal State-Space Learning for Robust Dynamic Facial Expression Recognition

Authors: Yudhistira Arditya Pratama, Yi-Zeng Hsieh

Abstract: Recognizing facial expressions in unconstrained video suffers from two coupled problems: self-attention grows quadratically in cost with clip length, and crowdsourced labels are noisy. We present GM-GReFEL, a spatiotemporal state-space network with a geometry-aware decision stage. Its Modulated GroupMamba backbone replaces a spatiotemporal Vision Transformer. Six channel groups are scanned separately, four over the frame plane and two over time, so the cost grows linearly with sequence length ($\mathcal{O}(N)$); after each layer, a Channel Affinity Modulation gate mixes the groups again. On top of the backbone, a GReFEL reliability module turns the normalized entropy of each prediction into a mixing weight between the classifier output and a vote from trainable geometric anchors, so that facial geometry counts for more when the network is unsure. The model is initialized by static-to-dynamic weight transfer and trained with a Triple Loss. In five-fold cross-validation, the best fold scores 66.57\%/76.46\% UAR/WAR on DFEW, 0.3 points or less below the strongest compared method, and 45.66\%/62.27\% on MAFW, ahead of all compared methods; on FERV39k the mean over five splits, 42.30\%/52.66\%, is competitive. The five-fold means are 64.07\%/74.54\% on DFEW, on par with recent large pre-trained models, and 38.90\%/54.37\% on MAFW, below the best of them. A paired $t$-test on DFEW indicates a modest benefit of temporal modeling (+1.61 UAR points, one-sided $p=0.031$). Attention stays on plausible facial regions, and latency is close to a ViT-B/16 baseline (47.4 vs.\ 46.8 ms per clip). Code: https://github.com/ardiytama/GroupMamba-DFER.

URLs: https://github.com/ardiytama/GroupMamba-DFER.

cross Prompt Sensitivity in Tool-Augmented Model Comparisons

Authors: Mateusz Modrzejewski, Tomasz Radzikowski

Abstract: Language models are compared on question-answering benchmarks that pose each question in one wording, and a reworded question can change which of two models scores higher. We ask whether giving the models tools makes such comparisons more stable. An accuracy gain cannot answer this, because tools change several steps between the question and the scored answer at once. We propose a matched protocol that separates those steps: the answer readout, reasoning, the decision to call, the tool's answer, and the report supplied without a call. We apply it to six open models in five domains under five wordings. We find that tools raise accuracy where a program answers the question and widen the wording sensitivity of many pairwise comparisons. The widening follows how often a model calls, and call frequency follows the wording for some models and not others. It also depends on the interface and the instructions: a native tool-calling interface and a second tool description leave no detected widening in molecules, and sensitivity persists where tools return intermediate results. These findings show that deterministic reports do not guarantee stable comparisons. We recommend reporting call frequency and comparison stability under several wordings beside accuracy.

cross Prediction Uncertainty Controlled Computer Model Calibration

Authors: Ryan Heejoon Byun, Dayeon Yoon, Won Chang

Abstract: Computer-model calibration is usually undertaken in service of prediction, yet neither Bayesian Kennedy-O'Hagan (KOH) calibration nor amortized simulation-based inference is built to control the predictive error of the calibrated simulator. When parameters are weakly identified or the data-model discrepancy is misspecified, the simulator-output bands induced by a nominal posterior level can be far from nominal. We propose a two-layer framework that targets predictive miscoverage directly. First, a conditional flow-matching model learns an amortized posterior for the calibration parameters, trained on simulator outputs perturbed with Gaussian-process discrepancies of varying structure. Second, conformal risk control arranges posterior draws for the target observation into nested candidate sets and selects the smallest one whose fixed-rank pointwise bands attain a prescribed time-averaged miscoverage on a synthetic calibration sample. Under exchangeability of calibration and target observations, the bands control expected miscoverage in finite samples. In a synthetic benchmark, the proposed bands are roughly 20% the width of KOH-MCMC bands with miscoverage near the 10% target. In calibrating an age-structured SEIR model to the 2010 Malawi measles outbreak, they are again sharper and near-nominal, and the weeks they fail to cover coincide with the asymmetric epidemic decline, exposing structural inadequacy of the mechanistic model.

cross Memory before Goal: Role of Pre-History Memory for Humanoid Loco-Manipulation

Authors: Chenyuan Chen, Ran Ding, Lechen Zhang, Zewen He, Yoshihiko Nakamura

Abstract: Humanoid loco-manipulation in dynamic scenes is non-Markovian: cues vanish as objects change or are hidden, or leave the view as the robot turns and walks, yet whole-body vision-language-action (VLA) policies see only the present. We present WholeBodyMem, an object-guided perceptual memory that keeps the pre-history of object events seen before the robot acts on its goal. SAM3 tracks the task objects, and their events causally select key frames within an adaptive budget, which the action expert reads by cross-attention together with object tokens encoding appearance, geometry and time. We also contribute HuMeGo, a humanoid memory benchmark with six simulated and six real-world Unitree G1 tasks. Keeping the right frames rather than more frames, WholeBodyMem surpasses memoryless and uniform-memory baselines, raising its base policy's success from 29.1% to 72.6% in simulation and from 36.7% to 88.3% on the real robot, while also improving the standard SIMPLE tasks (70.2% to 86.9%).

cross Prompting Strategy Effects Depend on the Judge: A Multilingual Study of LLM-as-a-Judge Robustness

Authors: Asal Mehrabi, Mohammad Aliannejadi, Clemencia Siro

Abstract: LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation is increasingly applied to multilingual tasks, yet prompting strategies such as Chain-of-Thought and Role Conditioning are recommended based solely on English benchmarks. Whether their effectiveness transfers across judge models and languages remains unknown: prompt-sensitivity studies are largely English-centric, while existing multilingual judge studies fix the prompt template. In this work, we address this gap by measuring this interaction through a benchmark of 9 prompting strategies, 4 open-weight judges, 27 languages, and 4 tasks across 13 datasets, yielding over 1M pointwise scores on gold-reference outputs, validated against human judgments. We find that prompting strategies are not transferable: the choice of the judge model dominates the choice of prompting strategy, strategy effects reverse direction across judges, and stability degrades in low-resource languages, where these effects are concealed by aggregate reporting. These findings show that prompting effects in multilingual evaluation are conditioned on the judge, task, and language. Consequently, prompting strategies should be validated for the intended evaluation setting, and cross-study comparisons should account for both judge and prompt configuration.

Abstract: Modern high energy nuclear and particle physics experiments produce large amounts of sparse data that must be reduced significantly in size before performing physics analysis. cross RaSFNet: Ray-aligned and Scan-aware Fusion Network for LiDAR Semantic Segmentation

Authors: Chuang Chen

Abstract: LiDAR point cloud semantic segmentation is a fundamental component of 3D perception for autonomous driving and robotics. Projection-based methods map 3D point clouds into 2D representations for feature extraction using 2D networks. However, aggregating multiple points during projection can obscure geometric differences within each frustum, while local feature extraction limits the modeling of spatial relationships across scanning regions. Moreover, proximity in the projected representation does not necessarily imply proximity in 3D space, and interactions between point and projected features may mix features from different ranges, reducing semantic discriminability. To address these challenges, we propose the Ray-aligned and Scan-aware Fusion Network (RaSFNet), which incorporates point cloud geometry and LiDAR scanning characteristics into projection-based semantic segmentation. Specifically, the Ray-Aligned Geometric Distribution Encoding (RAGDE) module characterizes point distributions within each frustum using a ray-aligned coordinate system. To model spatial context, a Scan-Topology-Aware Mamba (STAM) block organizes frustum features according to the LiDAR azimuth sampling pattern and captures long-range dependencies across scanning regions. In addition, a Range-Constrained Point-Frustum Fusion (RCPFF) mechanism uses shared range information to guide feature exchange between point and frustum representations while maintaining spatial correspondence. RaSFNet achieves mIoU scores of 70.2% and 77.7% on the SemanticKITTI and nuScenes validation sets, respectively, indicating competitive segmentation performance compared with the evaluated methods.

cross When More Metadata Misleads: Probing Field-Level Grounding in LLM-Generated Museum Descriptions

Authors: Clemencia Siro, Laura Hollink

Abstract: Metadata-conditioned generation is often framed as a data-to-text task in which additional input fields should improve grounding. Museum description generation challenges this assumption because catalogue records are heterogeneous, partially visual, and unevenly documented across geographic regions. We study how metadata composition shapes the generation of artefact descriptions through a two-phase framework. Phase 1 analyses natural metadata variation in a large museum collection, where field count, field type, image availability, and Place co-vary. Phase 2 introduces controlled field probes that hold object identity fixed while varying metadata quantity, field type, and image access. Our results show that metadata quantity alone does not explain generation behaviour. Field type determines whether models ground, omit, or infer information, while images can both support visual grounding and increase unsupported interpretation. More broadly, our findings show that metadata-conditioned data-to-text evaluation should assess field- and claim-level support, rather than relying only on aggregate input coverage or reference similarity.

cross Muting Tokens: Exploring Token Sparsification for Efficient Audio Transformers

Authors: Shreya Sajid, Fabrizio Julian Piva, Hiram Rayo Torres Rodriguez, Elif Ceren Gok Yildirim, Murat Onur Yildirim, Joaquin Vanschoren

Abstract: Transformer-based models have shown strong performance across diverse tasks, but their reliance on self-attention mechanisms often leads to high computational costs. Token sparsification, which reduces the number of tokens processed in transformer layers, offers a promising solution to maintain performance while improving efficiency. However, although widely studied in the vision and language domains, token sparsification remains underexplored in audio, with existing work often limited to a single method, model size, or dataset. This work presents a comprehensive evaluation of token sparsification techniques for audio transformers. We assess a range of token pruning and merging strategies across multiple datasets and model sizes, under both post-training and fine-tuning settings. Furthermore, we introduce a novel sparsification approach that combines attention scores with spectral cues derived from the frequency content of token embeddings. Our experiments show that frequency-aware approaches, particularly those based on spectral flatness and low-frequency energy, consistently outperform attention-only methods. We also find that sparsification based on spectrogram patch intensity is especially effective for longer audio inputs. These results highlight the value of incorporating frequency-domain and input-specific information into sparsification strategies to enhance performance in audio tasks.

cross Can Entanglement-Induced Feature-Map Designs Improve Projected Quantum Kernels?

Authors: Ravi Kumar Jha, Hsuan-Yin Lin, Eirik Rosnes

Abstract: Projected quantum kernels (PQKs) have emerged as a notable approach to circumvent the hardness of machine learning tasks arising from the exponential dimensionality of the quantum Hilbert space. However, recent PQK studies often rely heavily on extensive classical hyperparameter tuning of the radial basis function (RBF) kernel parameter and the support vector machine regularization parameter. Authors: Mateusz Odrowaz-Sypniewski, Michael Amir, Jasmine Bayrooti, Ajay Shankar, Amanda Prorok

Abstract: Real robot data, rich across tasks and morphologies, is often treated as the bottleneck for training robot learning models, including embodied foundation models that aim for zero-shot physical transfer. cross Beyond Accuracy: Disentangling Decomposition from Typed-State Quality Assurance in Multi-Agent LLM Pipelines

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM pipelines are often credited with gains in software engineering tasks, but it is rarely clear whether a gain stems from the typed contract between agents or from the specialized prompts that decomposition enables. Abstract: During the deep learning era, innovations in remote sensing (RS) vision models primarily focused on optimizing network architectures for specific tasks and conducting end-to-end training. cross AgenticEvolve: Evolutionary Program Search with Persistent Language Model Agents that Design and Run Experiments

Authors: Ze-Yu Peng, Qing-Yu Lan, Zhan-He Wang, Jun Zhang, Yun-Song Piao

Abstract: Evolutionary program search with large language models keeps the structure of an evolutionary algorithm and uses the model as its variation operator. Typically, the model generates each candidate program in a single request from parent programs and their scores. We present AgenticEvolve, an evolutionary program search framework in which each island is run by a persistent large language model agent. Between evaluations, the agent designs and runs experiments on the candidate program. On two heuristic design tasks with three large language models, AgenticEvolve produces better programs than No-analysis and OpenEvolve on both hidden and unseen instances. cross Deep Learning for Random Objects in Metric Spaces: Beyond Manifold Assumptions

Authors: Marcos Matabuena, Xiaokai Luo, Oscar Hernan Madrid Padilla

Abstract: Deep learning (DL) methods have substantially improved predictive performance across a wide range of downstream prediction tasks, enabling new discoveries in multiple domains. However, principled DL methods for non-Euclidean random objects remain relatively scarce, despite the growing importance of modern biomedical outcomes taking the form of probability distributions, random graphs, functions, and other random objects in metric spaces. Motivated by a new generation of digital and clinical biomarkers, we propose a deep learning framework for regression with random objects in metric spaces. Our approach operates directly on the metric structure of the response space, avoiding restrictive manifold or Euclidean embedding assumptions imposed by many existing methods. At the same time, it retains modern statistical guarantees under the stated statistical assumptions. We establish statistical consistency and convergence rates for the proposed estimator, and characterize conditions under which it can mitigate the curse of dimensionality for hierarchically compositional regression functions. Extensive simulation studies demonstrate competitive predictive performance, with gains over several competing approaches in multiple Euclidean and non-Euclidean configurations. Finally, we demonstrate the practical utility of the proposed framework in two digital health case studies with distribution-valued outcomes in Wasserstein spaces, using high-resolution accelerometry and continuous glucose monitoring data. Embedding-based deep-learning methods may be less suitable when no informative low-dimensional representation exists or when the learned embedding does not adequately preserve the intrinsic geometry of the response space.

cross Metric Conformal Prediction via the Conditional Expected Local Radius

Authors: Rui Qiu, Fang Yao, Zhou Yu

Abstract: We introduce the conditional expected local radius (CELR) and develop a new conformal prediction method for non-Euclidean responses in general metric spaces. CELR measures local conditional probability concentration, allowing prediction sets to accommodate heterogeneity and multimodality without requiring densities or a vector space structure. Its conditional distribution yields a nonconformity score that is comparable across covariate values and avoids costly transport comparisons between distance profiles. We further adapt a metric distributional random forest that handles multivariate covariates and nonlinear interactions, while a single forest fit supplies all score components, reducing computation by eliminating repeated local fitting. We establish uniform consistency of the estimators and asymptotic conditional validity under suitable regularity conditions, together with finite-sample marginal coverage under exchangeability. Numerical studies demonstrate effective uncertainty quantification across different metric spaces and substantial computational savings over the conditional profile score method.

cross Graph-Recurrent Learning of Emergent Collective Dynamics

Authors: Ali Rayat, Acacia Lee, Gia-Wei Chern

Abstract: Collective ordering in correlated electron systems emerges from microscopic dynamics, yet the evolution of observable textures need not admit a closed, memoryless description. We introduce a graph-recurrent neural network (GRNN) framework that learns such emergent dynamics directly from trajectories without specifying microscopic equations of motion. The framework integrates gated recurrent memory with graph message passing: local hidden states encode information beyond the instantaneous observables, while spatial coupling allows this information to propagate between sites. The observable and hidden fields evolve jointly through a shared local rule, providing a scalable representation of dynamics with spatial interactions and temporal memory. We demonstrate this framework on the emergent dynamics of charge-density-wave order in the semiclassical Holstein model, successfully reproducing temporal and spatial correlations and dynamical scaling during domain coarsening. By learning finite-time evolution directly, the GRNN offers a route to efficient, data-driven descriptions of collective dynamics with unresolved microscopic degrees of freedom.

cross AfterDeleteBench: Auditing Post-Deletion Information Persistence in LLM Agents

Authors: Ehsan Saleh, Roozbeh Nahavandi, Sama Salarian, Raef Bassily, Andrew Perrault

Abstract: Many LLM agents maintain external persistent memory and derive additional state from past interactions, such as summaries. Deleting an original memory record therefore need not remove information carried by its descendants. We introduce AfterDeleteBench, a controlled behavioral audit for this failure mode in fixed-weight LLM agents. For each synthetic secret, we compare an exposed-then-deleted agent with an otherwise matched agent that was never exposed to the secret, allowing post-deletion recovery to be attributed to the original exposure. In our primary setting, a single query recovers the deleted secret from Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct in roughly two thirds of cases, while matched never-exposed controls have zero recovery. Crucially, recovery does not require a surviving verbatim copy: when the complete secret string is absent and only separately stored components remain under a public composition rule, exact reconstruction succeeds in 62-99% of cases across Qwen, Mistral, and Phi-3. We also show that the response interface is part of the measurement system: target-bearing replies can be scored as failures when the requested format and parser are misaligned. Finally, we separate reconstruction from write-back and fresh-session recall. Provenance-aware closure eliminates observed recovery in the tested positive-baseline settings, whereas an exact-literal write filter blocks reinsertion but cannot erase information already present. Together, these results show that source deletion should be evaluated behaviorally at the level of the agent and its memory system, not only as a storage operation.

cross Convex Equilibrium Potentials for Monge--Amp\`ere: Curvature Bounds and A Posteriori Evaluation

Authors: Edison V\'azquez, Yingdong Lu, Tomasz Nowicki

Abstract: Equilibrium neural networks define their hidden state as the fixed point of a contraction, so their effective depth is set at evaluation time rather than by the architecture. Using them for fully nonlinear PDEs raises two issues that explicit networks avoid: second derivatives may grow with the effective depth, and the potential is only ever evaluated approximately. We study nonnegative softplus equilibrium networks with contraction factor $q<1$, a convex class suited to the Monge--Amp\`ere equation. A positivity cancellation in the resolvent shows that the Hessian grows at most like $(1-q)^{-2}$, rather than the $(1-q)^{-3}$ of a direct estimate, and an explicit one-neuron family shows that this exponent is sharp; third derivatives satisfy a sharp $(1-q)^{-3}$ bound. Residual-based a posteriori estimates then control the state, its input derivatives and, in two dimensions, the Hessian determinant at an approximate equilibrium. Combined with interval arithmetic on complete covers of the domain, they yield rigorous uniform bounds on the Monge--Amp\`ere residual and, through a comparison argument, on the solution error of a fixed trained network. On two manufactured Dirichlet problems on the unit disk, all six trained models are certified with residual below $0.25$ and boundary error below $0.01$. Adaptive stopping halves float64 evaluation time at best, whereas certified adaptive evaluation remains costlier than fixed depth.

cross HERD: Harness-Evolving Recursive Distillation for Continual Learning

Authors: Xun Sun, Thanh Thi Nguyen, Thuy-Trang Vu

Abstract: Continual learning requires a model to keep acquiring new skills and knowledge without compromising its existing capabilities. Parametric methods suffer from catastrophic forgetting and loss of plasticity. Non-parametric methods, such as prompts, skills, and harnesses, leverage in-context learning but are limited by context length and lossy plain-text compression. To effectively combine their complementary strengths, we propose HERD (Harness-Evolving Recursive Distillation), a hybrid continual learning framework that learns efficiently in the harness and internalizes experience into cumulative capabilities via distillation. On the harness side, HERD continually refines its skills to capture reusable patterns, while retaining high-residual examples that the skills fail to capture in a bounded memory. On the parameter side, HERD performs drift-constrained distillation with dual anchors, substantially reducing forgetting over long recursive runs. On the 25-stage task stream of TRACE+17, under fixed memory and compute budgets, HERD matches the strongest parametric baseline in new-task acquisition while exhibiting the least forgetting and the strongest retention of general capabilities.

cross How Depth Growth and Looping Shape In-Context Learning in Language Models

Authors: Ferdinand Kapl, Kaitlin Maile, Stefan Bauer, Johannes von Oswald

Abstract: Looped and depth-grown language models reuse computations across depth and have attracted interest for their reasoning capabilities. Building on prior suggestive evidence of improved in-context-learning (ICL), we investigate how such reuse favors ICL, including how it shapes the acquisition, retention, and mechanisms of this ability. Our analysis spans pretrained language models up to 3B parameters and controlled tasks. The experiments provide evidence for a shared inductive bias toward learning from context: grown and looped models rely more on information in context and, compared with a full-depth dense reference, acquire ICL earlier in controlled tasks and retain it more strongly when learning fixed associations in the weights is also possible. Tracing attention heads through training reveals that depth growth propagates early induction heads across layers. Ablations show that grown and looped models rely more strongly on induction-ranked heads for both few-shot performance and later-token prediction. Together, these findings provide evidence for a shared inductive bias toward learning from context and suggest that (soft) parameter reuse shapes how this ability develops and which attention mechanisms support it.

cross Dimension-free regimes for unadjusted Langevin in ridge-separable models: A subspace robust Wasserstein analysis

Authors: Yuansi Chen, Francesco Pedrotti, Peter A. Whalley

Abstract: The Unadjusted Langevin Algorithm (ULA) is widely used for high-dimensional Bayesian inference. Classical analyses in Wasserstein-2 distance predict an asymptotic bias and a mixing time that grow with dimension, but this metric is stronger than necessary for many statistical tasks. We instead analyze the bias in subspace robust Wasserstein distance, which controls the mean and arbitrary low-dimensional marginals and is therefore well suited to observables typically estimated in practice. For ridge-separable potentials formed with i.i.d. data, we prove high-probability bounds showing a bell-shaped mixing profile as the number of data points $N$ varies relative to the dimension $d$. In the overparameterized regime, the effective dimension is the rank $N$ of the data-dependent perturbation, leading to complexity depending on $N$ rather than $d$. In the large-sample regime, Gaussian-perturbation estimates yield dimension-free complexity once $N$ is sufficiently large. In contrast, near the proportional regime $\asymp d$, we prove that dimension-free guarantees are impossible in general.

cross Conservative Meta-Agent Search: Architecture Adaptation under Non-Stationarity

Authors: Ali Atiah Alzahrani

Abstract: Automated agentic-system design is typically evaluated on static task suites with immediate scalar rewards. Deployed systems face persistent shift, delayed dependent feedback, correlated component failures, and costly architecture changes. We formalize meta-agent architecture search as sequential incumbent replacement with switching costs and hard risk constraints and introduce Conservative Meta-Agent Search (CMAS). CMAS retrieves archived architectures and proposes typed graph edits, then scores each against the incumbent by paired shadow replay under common random numbers, with interventional component credit. Promotion requires an empirically calibrated, variance-penalized simultaneous score above a pre-specified margin, and is followed by a simulated canary stage and pre-specified rollback. Since backtests cannot certify meta-level skill, we introduce MetaInvest-Bench, a regime-switching simulator whose specified worker competence makes a surrogate-optimal architecture computable, with leakage-controlled replay, adversarial governance tracks, and five negative controls. Across 96 streams, CMAS cuts normalized mean surrogate dynamic-oracle regret from 0.116 to 0.104 and raises held-out utility from 0.44 to 0.48 over the strongest adaptive baseline; when extra workers add no value, it contracts to near-single-agent teams. Its deployment inference cost is ~6% lower, though its search-inclusive token cost is the highest of the methods compared. The observed per-update false-promotion rate is 3.4% against a nominal 5% target, and 4.8% in a sealed rerun. The calibration is empirical: it concerns an update-level replay statistic on an author-designed benchmark, and guarantees neither full-utility improvement, nor absence of simulated canary-stage harm, nor sequence-level error control.

cross DIAL: Steerable Language Models with Distilled Instruction-Adherence Levels

Authors: Thanh Q. Tran, John Russell Himawan, Arun Verma, Kiwan Wong, Wei Xiao, Daniela Rus, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low

Abstract: When a user says "Keep it short", how short should the LLM's response be? More generally, users and applications require different levels of instruction adherence, but prompting does not reliably provide fine-grained control over them. We therefore study "steerable language models" that provide fine-grained numerical control over the degree to which models follow general instructions specified in text. However, existing approaches do not jointly offer fine-grained control, efficiency, and scalability across diverse instructions. To address this gap, we introduce DIAL (Distilled Instruction-Adherence Levels) to achieve all three properties. For fine-grained control, we perform classifier-free guidance (CFG) with a frozen LLM to provide training signals for on-policy distillation of instruction adherence at different levels. For efficiency, we design a lightweight steering adapter that uses a classifier over the LLM's hidden states to compute a closed-form steering edit. The classifier adaptively sets the steering magnitude at each token based on the target adherence level, while accounting for the geometry of the LLM's hidden states to preserve response coherence. For scalability, the classifier is text-conditioned, allowing a shared adapter to support new instructions without per-instruction fitting, and training requires only instruction and request text. Evaluation across multiple LLM families shows that DIAL achieves a competitive balance between concept expression and response fluency, with concept scores increasing monotonically across adherence levels. We introduce SteerIF, an instruction-only dataset of 98, 625 concept, persona, verifiable, and descriptive instructions. When trained on SteerIF, DIAL provides fine-grained control over personas and, when combined with prompting, improves verifiable instruction following.

Authors: Khaled Waleed Dawoud, Muhammad Zaigham Zaheer, Khan Muhammad, Muhammad Haris Khan

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have shown strong potential for video anomaly detection, but dense frame-level inference makes them impractical for real-time deployment. Existing acceleration commonly relies on uniform sampling, which ignores content dynamics and can either waste computation on static segments or miss localized anomalies. On XD-Violence, we improve over the previous best result by +0.57% while delivering a 1.31x inference speedup. These results demonstrate that content-aware allocation of VLM capacity, processing fewer frames more intelligently rather than all frames uniformly, is a more effective axis of progress than uniformly densifying VLM inference across frames. Code is available on GitHub: https://github.com/kdawoud91/MoG-VLM.

cross Structure-preserving reduced-order models of thin panels in supersonic and hypersonic flow

Authors: Miguel A. Aguilo

Abstract: We build structure-preserving reduced-order models for both challenge problems of the AePW-4 High-Speed Working Group. They are a clamped thin panel at Mach 1.92 (RC-19) and a cantilever plate at Mach 5.85 (HyMAX). The published data for each case hold no time-resolved response and no repeated runs. It does so without that model's 113 Hz spectral peak, which the measurements do not show. Measured displacements can also correct the chaotic response through one more force that respects the energy balance. We tested this on synthetic data at the experiment's 5 kHz sampling rate. The state error is 1.25% of the panel thickness without noise and 3.5% with 10 micrometers of noise. cross Nonparametric autoregressive estimation of probability distributions with parallel gradient boosting

Authors: R\'emy Chapelle (CB, EVDG, CESP)

Abstract: Nonparametric estimation of joint probability distributions is a core problem in statistics, which is notoriously challenging with high-dimensional data. Such challenges also arise with conditional distribution estimation and have led to flexible algorithms that aim to tackle the ''curse of dimensionality'' by exploiting predictive structures of lower dimension than the ambient space. In this paper, we study the use of such conditional distribution estimators to estimate joint distributions by factorizing the latter in an ''autoregressive'' way. We show that for loss functions satisfying a subadditivity property, bounds on the convergence rate of an autoregressive estimator can be derived from bounds on the convergence rate of the conditional distribution estimators. These bounds relate the difficulty of estimating the joint distribution to that of estimating the most challenging factor in the autoregressive factorization. When the conditional estimators adapt to sparsity in their conditioning sets, we characterize this difficulty through variable elimination in an undirected dependence graph. This allows bounding the rates of convergence by quantities related to the sparsity of the graph, and provides an additional motivation for the autoregressive approach. We then evaluate the practical performance of an autoregressive estimator based on parallel gradient boosting, a flexible nonparametric method for estimating conditional distributions. The estimator demonstrates competitive estimation performance on various simulated and real datasets, even without optimizing the factorization order. Together, these results support nonparametric autoregressive estimation as a practical approach to flexible joint distribution estimation.

cross Direct Inference on Admissible Sets (Diona)

Authors: Lorenzo Sabug Jr., Eric Kerrigan

Abstract: We propose a new regression method named Direct Inference on Admissible Sets (Diona) that directly uses samples to estimate the state and its derivatives at a query point, along with their admissible bounds, without long global training. By casting the values and derivatives at the query and neighbouring samples as latent variables in a constrained optimisation problem, the burden of long training loops becomes one of on-demand local solves. We establish Diona's theoretical properties including finite-sample coverage and, in the noiseless case, convergence to the true state and derivatives under dense sampling. In a nonlinear regression benchmark on six functions of three to eight dimensions, Diona was more accurate than a Gaussian process (GP) on noiseless data especially with denser data sets. Furthermore, as with GP, it produced uncertainty bounds that contained the true value at more than 95% of query points in four functions. Diona's time to a first prediction after a data set update was three to four orders of magnitude shorter than that of the GP, and two to three orders shorter than that of a neural network, which was more accurate but offers no uncertainty quantification. This low latency is paid for by on-demand predictions, so this latency advantage applies when relatively few predictions are needed between data updates.

Abstract: Local anomaly detection is the task of identifying spatially confined anomalous or out-of-distribution (OOD) regions within an otherwise in-distribution (ID) image. Abstract: Accuracy on medical examinations is common evidence offered for the clinical use of language models, yet a health system that intends to run an open model on its own hardware needs to know considerably more: whether the model recognizes when it is likely to be wrong, whether medical fine-tuning improves this ability, whether its correct answers rest on sound clinical reasoning, or whether it actually uses the images it is given. cross Beyond Best-of-$k$: Prior-Consistent DAPS for Ambiguous Nonlinear Inverse Problems

Authors: Merve Kocabas, Benjamin A. Burns, Andreas Geiger, Kent Gauen, Sara Fridovich-Keil

Abstract: Diffusion models have become strong priors for solving inverse problems. However, nonlinear inverse problems such as phase retrieval remain challenging when multiple measurement-consistent solutions are distinguishable only by the prior; we call such measurements ambiguous. Existing diffusion-based samplers rely on local approximations during reconstruction. For an ambiguous measurement, these approximations can allow measurement guidance to favor a measurement-consistent mode that is close to a prior-supported mode but is itself not supported by the prior. Evaluation often masks this type of failure by reporting the best-of-$k$ runs, where the best is selected by comparison to the ground truth. We introduce Prior-Consistent DAPS (PC-DAPS), a variant of Decoupled Annealing Posterior Sampling (DAPS) designed to address this failure case by maintaining multiple candidate solutions within a single sampling run. As annealing noise decreases, PC-DAPS prunes and replaces weaker candidates according to their prior consistency, without any ground-truth access. PC-DAPS reduces the incidence of prior-inconsistent samples and improves sample quality on nonlinear inverse problems while preserving sample quality and diversity on linear inverse problems.

cross EpiSEM-Bench: Evaluating Safety and Evidence Recovery in LLM Agents under Missing Evidence

Abstract: Tool-using language model agents rely on environmental facts to decide whether an action is permitted. When a tool response omits a safety-relevant fact, an agent may carry out a prohibited action even though the user request is benign and every returned statement is true. The benchmark reveals substantial variation across the ten models in omission robustness, evidence acquisition, and appropriate action. Resistance to missing evidence does not necessarily translate into useful recovery. Recovery access reduces unsafe execution for all ten models, but agents differ in whether they actively obtain the missing facts and use them to act appropriately in both worlds.

cross Reliable AI-Assisted Decisions via Selective Auditing

Authors: Isaac Gibbs, Elise L. Han, Ryan J. Tibshirani, Tijana Zrnic

Abstract: We consider online monitoring of an AI system that produces a sequence of decisions. At each round, the system outputs a proposed decision together with a confidence score estimating the trustworthiness of the decision. The decision and confidence score need not be produced by the same model: for example, one agent may propose a decision, while a separate verifier agent evaluates it and outputs a correctness or safety score. Based on this information, we must decide whether to accept the AI decision automatically or consult an expert, such as a human auditor, who reviews the proposal and either accepts or corrects it. cross Phase Transition from Random Features to Fully Trainable Networks

Authors: Xin He, Huan Lao, Xiliang Lu, Shijun Zhang

Abstract: Constructing accurate and computationally tractable approximation spaces is a fundamental task in scientific computing. Partially trainable neural networks bridge fixed random features and fully adaptive representations. This paper quantifies a phase transition pattern in approximation bounds and observes analogous behavior in training errors: a rapid but smooth initial improvement followed by diminishing returns, with a sharper transition under greater initialization mismatch. Under polynomial spectral decay with exponent $\alpha>1$ and leverage compatibility, we establish a squared $L_2$ approximation rate $\mathcal{O}((\kappa_P\log(m/\delta)/m)^\alpha)$ for two-layer networks of width $m$ and trainable proportion $P$, where $\kappa_P=(P/\kappa_1^*+(1-P)/\kappa_0^*)^{-1}$. The bound holds uniformly over the unit ball of a reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) with probability at least $1-\delta$. The constants $\kappa_0^*$ and $\kappa_1^*$ quantify the fixed and trainable groups' sampling mismatches relative to the target RKHS. For spherical biased-ReLU features in dimension $d$, we verify these assumptions with $\alpha=(d+2)/(d-1)$ and $\kappa_0^*=4\sqrt{2}\tau(d+1)/d$ for initialization scale $\tau\ge1$, and $\kappa_1^*=1$ under ridge leverage sampling. For $d=5$ and $\tau=10$, a trainable proportion of approximately $4.1\%$ captures $90\%$ of the reduction in the approximation bound from $P=0$ to $P=1$. A structured three-layer example illustrates how a few trainable first-layer neurons exploit fixed downstream blocks through nonlinear composition. Numerical experiments across diverse settings, including networks with up to 15 layers, exhibit similar phase transitions in training errors.

Abstract: Robotic foundation models are typically evaluated through costly closed-loop rollouts, which motivates cheap offline proxies for model capability. The Platonic Representation Hypothesis (PRH) suggests that as representations improve, different modalities such as vision and language converge toward a shared statistical model of reality. We ask whether motion trajectories form another such modality: as embodied models become more capable, should their sensory representations converge toward the structure of physical motion? replace On Sex, Evolution, and the Multiplicative Weights Update Algorithm

Authors: Reshef Meir, David Parkes

Abstract: We consider a recent innovative theory by Chastain et al. on the role of sex in evolution [PNAS'14]. In short, the theory suggests that the evolutionary process of gene recombination implements the celebrated multiplicative weights updates algorithm (MWUA). They prove that the population dynamics induced by sexual reproduction can be precisely modeled by genes that use MWUA as their learning strategy in a particular coordination game. The result holds in the environments of \emph{weak selection}, under the assumption that the population frequencies remain a product distribution. We revisit the theory, eliminating both the requirement of weak selection and any assumption on the distribution of the population. Removing the assumption of product distributions is crucial, since as we show, this assumption is inconsistent with the population dynamics. We show that the marginal allele distributions induced by the population dynamics precisely match the marginals induced by a multiplicative weights update algorithm in this general setting, thereby affirming and substantially generalizing these earlier results. We further revise the implications for convergence and utility or fitness guarantees in coordination games. In contrast to the claim of Chastain et al.[PNAS'14], we conclude that the sexual evolutionary dynamics does not entail any property of the population distribution, beyond those already implied by convergence.

Authors: Johannes Burchert, Thorben Werner, Vijaya Krishna Yalavarthi, Diego Coello de Portugal, Maximilian Stubbemann, Lars Schmidt-Thieme

Abstract: As with most other data domains, EEG data analysis relies on rich domain-specific preprocessing. For EEG classification many models have been developed with layer types and architectures we typically do not see in time series classification. replace The Fenchel-Young Generalization of Bayesian Principles

Authors: Sophia Sklaviadis, Thomas Moellenhoff, Emtiyaz Khan, Andre Martins, Mario Figueiredo

Abstract: Conjugate Bayes plays a key role in machine learning, ensuring computationally simple posterior updates. We generalize the variational principle behind this computation by using the Fenchel-Young (FY) inequality, which lets us define a generalized evidence lower bound beyond the KL geometry, and at equality, characterizes new generalized posteriors. A generalized notion of conjugacy follows, defined as closure of prior-potential families that are affine under translation by the loss. We show that such families not only preserve additive canonical updating, but also posterior inference with parameter-dependent supports and tuneably heavy tails. This combination is not feasible with standard conjugate Bayes. We derive a new FY generalization of expectation maximization (EM), which recovers classical and Hard EM, and amortized versions. We show examples, including generalized Bayesian linear regression with closed-form bounded support and heavy-tailed posteriors, and a Gaussian mixture with sparse cluster assignments.

replace Fragility-aware Classification for Risk Characterization and Robust Learning

Authors: Zheng Cui, Daniel Zhuoyu Long, Jin Qi, Chen Yang, Ruohan Zhan

Abstract: Classification models play a central role in data-driven decision-making applications such as medical diagnosis, recommendation systems, and risk assessment. Traditional performance metrics, such as accuracy and AUC, quantify classification errors and pairwise ranking performance but do not account for the magnitude of errors, i.e., the risk of confident misjudgments. This limitation is particularly consequential in safety-critical and cost-sensitive settings, where overconfident errors can lead to severe outcomes. To address this issue, we propose the Fragility Index (FI), a novel performance metric that evaluates classifiers from a risk-averse perspective by capturing the tail risk of confident misjudgments. We formulate FI within a robust satisficing (RS) framework to quantify classifier sensitivity under distributional uncertainty. Building on this, we develop a tractable surrogate-based training framework and establish provable bounds connecting training fragility to ranking-error FI. We further derive tractable reformulations for a broad class of loss functions, including cross-entropy, hinge-type, and Lipschitz losses, and identify specific conditions under which these reformulations are exact. We also introduce an FI-induced regularizer for deep neural networks. Empirical results on real-world medical diagnosis tasks demonstrate that FI complements existing metrics by revealing error tail risk not reflected in accuracy or AUC. FI-based models achieve competitive predictive performance while generally attaining lower FI. In the simulated human--AI diagnostic workflow, they also achieve lower average operational costs than empirical risk minimization.

replace Multi-View Causal Discovery without Non-Gaussianity: Identifiability and Algorithms

Authors: Ambroise Heurtebise, Omar Chehab, Pierre Ablin, Alexandre Gramfort, Aapo Hyv\"arinen

Abstract: Causal discovery is a difficult problem that typically relies on strong assumptions on the data-generating model, such as non-Gaussianity. In practice, many modern applications provide multiple related views of the same system, which has rarely been considered for causal discovery. Here, we leverage this multi-view structure to achieve causal discovery with weak assumptions. We propose a multi-view linear Structural Equation Model (SEM) that extends the well-known framework of non-Gaussian disturbances by alternatively leveraging correlation over views. We prove the identifiability of the model for acyclic SEMs. Subsequently, we propose several multi-view causal discovery algorithms, inspired by single-view algorithms (DirectLiNGAM, PairwiseLiNGAM, and ICA-LiNGAM). The new methods are validated through simulations and applications on neuroimaging data, where they enable the estimation of causal graphs between brain regions.

replace ACTIVA: Amortized Causal Effect Estimation via Variational Autoencoders

Authors: Andreas Sauter, Saber Salehkaleybar, Frank van Harmelen, Aske Plaat, Erman Acar

Abstract: Predicting post-intervention distributions from observational data is central to many scientific and decision-making problems, but remains challenging due to causal ambiguity, restrictive modeling assumptions, and the lack of amortization across tasks. We introduce ACTIVA, a transformer-based conditional variational autoencoder for amortized estimation of full interventional distributions from observational data and intervention queries. ACTIVA learns a conditional latent prior that amortizes causal knowledge across diverse training tasks, so that a new causal model drawn from the training distribution is handled in a single forward pass, without interventional data, a causal graph, or per-task fitting. Beyond the training distribution, only a parametric shift of the simulator is tested. We provide a population-level characterization of ACTIVA's estimand, showing that, under idealized expressivity and infinite samples assumptions, ACTIVA's learning objective targets a simulator-weighted mixture over the interventional distributions of causal models that are observationally compatible with the input. Empirically, on synthetic datasets and biologically realistic gene-expression simulations, ACTIVA substantially outperforms a correlational baseline, reduces spurious non-descendant effects, and achieves competitive performance relative to strong amortized baselines. On a pair of observationally equivalent models sharing a single observational dataset, ACTIVA recovers the predicted mixture, validating the theory in a finite-capacity model. Our results show that ACTIVA is a promising approach for estimating interventional distributions from observational data.

replace The Mathematical Theory of Double Descent as Shrinkage, not Overfitting: Matching Bounds for Path-Regularized Multilayer Neural Networks

Authors: Hao Yu

Abstract: We give a nonasymptotic generalization theory for multilayer fully connected neural networks trained with path regularization, for general Lipschitz losses and without assuming the loss bounded, the architecture sparse, or any width or depth limit. Approximation error is modelled explicitly, and along the way we settle an open problem of E et al. on approximation rates in generalized Barron spaces. We treat the underparameterized and overparameterized regimes separately, which is what allows the resulting curve to be non-monotone in the width. We then prove lower bounds on the error itself, valid at every target and at every local minimizer, and combine them with the upper bounds into a theorem asserting that the error - not merely our bound on it - descends, rises and descends again as the width grows, with a peak exceeding the final plateau by a factor diverging in the sample size. Having both sides of the bound also identifies what drives the ascent, and it is not overfitting: once the regularization exceeds the dual norm of the noise, which the prescribed schedule does at every width, a slow-rate oracle inequality controls the error with no variance term at all. The rise is instead the shrinkage demanded by the regularization that the underparameterized regime requires, and we show it cannot be removed by any width-independent regularization under which the theory's guarantees hold. This mechanism is a property of the estimator analyzed here; we do not claim it accounts for the double descent reported for unregularized training.

replace Analysis of On-policy Policy Gradient Methods under the Distribution Mismatch

Authors: Weizhen Wang, Xinzhe Zhou, Jianping He, Xiaoming Duan

Abstract: Policy gradient methods are among the most successful approaches for solving challenging reinforcement learning problems. Despite their empirical success, many state-of-the-art policy gradient algorithms for discounted problems estimate update directions using a sampling distribution that differs from the discounted state distribution required by the policy gradient theorem. In this work, we analyze the impact of this distribution mismatch on policy gradient methods. Specifically, {for tabular direct and softmax parameterizations, we establish global convergence of objective values under biased updates}. {For tabular softmax policies, we further derive the exact leading-order coefficients of the asymptotic objective errors under biased and normalized exact updates. Their ratio provides an explicit criterion for determining when distribution mismatch accelerates or slows asymptotic convergence.} We then extend the analysis to more general policy parameterizations by deriving explicit bounds on both the state distribution mismatch and the induced normalized policy-gradient mismatch in episodic and continuing MDPs. These mismatches vanish at least linearly as the discount factor approaches one. Under an additional smoothness assumption, we further show that the limit inferior of the squared norm of the normalized exact policy-gradient direction along the biased iterates is bounded by $O((1-\gamma)^2)$ in both settings. Our results provide theoretical insights into the robustness of policy gradient methods to distribution mismatch and shed light on the gap between their theoretical formulations and practical implementations.

replace Does More Compute Make Every Adversarial Attack Transferable?

Authors: Greg Gluch, Shafi Goldwasser, Anastasiia Struss

Abstract: Transferable attacks, or transferable jailbreaks, are a particularly strong version of adversarial examples that fool all defenders in a class. In this work, we study when more compute enables transferable attacks. We show that, when there is a quadratic gap in computational resources between attacker and defender, every learnable classification task admits either a transferable attack or an adversarial defense. For generative tasks, this dichotomy fails below a quadratic gap, i.e., more compute for the attacker does not lead to a transferable attack. We prove a stronger result that implies this failure: a defense that spends additional computation after it sees the input can be provably strictly stronger than every defense that publishes a fixed model. This result provides formal evidence that increasing inference-time compute can improve robustness, as empirically observed by Zaremba et al. (2025). We assume the existence of an array of cryptographic primitives: identity-based fully homomorphic encryption, publicly verifiable zero-knowledge succinct non-interactive arguments of knowledge, non-parallelizing languages, and incrementally verifiable computation.

replace Aggregation of Published Non-Uniform Axial Power Data for Phase II of the OECD/NEA AI/ML Critical Heat Flux Benchmark

Abstract: Critical heat flux (CHF) marks the onset of boiling crisis in light-water reactors, defining safe thermal-hydraulic operating limits. To support Phase II of the OECD/NEA AI/ML CHF benchmark, which introduces spatially varying power profiles, this work compiles and digitizes a broad CHF dataset covering both uniform and non-uniform axial heating conditions. Heating profiles were extracted from technical reports, interpolated onto a consistent axial mesh, validated via energy-balance checks, and encoded in machine-readable formats for benchmark compatibility.
